Yes, definitely. It isn’t just one thing, like… if it were only one bill or one medical appointment or something, I could almost understand it. I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of myself as doing a lot of, you know, ritual-type checking. I mean, I might look back at something once or twice if I’m unsure, like a date or whether I wrote something down correctly, but I don’t know that I repeatedly check things over and over after I truly know they’re handled.

So even if something is technically taken care of, I may still keep thinking, “But what if I missed something?” I don’t always go and check again, though. Sometimes I just sit with that uneasy feeling and go over it mentally, which is frustrating in its own way.

It just keeps running in the background, and sometimes right in the foreground, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly one specific problem?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It isn’t just one thing, like… if it were only one bill or one medical appointment or something, I could almost understand it. But my mind sort of wanders from one concern to the next. I’ll be thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether everything is lined up properly, and then somehow I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans, whether she’ll make connections safely, whether something will go wrong, and then I’ll notice the weather and think, oh, the gutters on the house probably need attention, and what if that turns into damage, and then that becomes a whole other thread.\n\nIt’s not always enormous things at first. Sometimes it starts with something fairly ordinary, but in my head it begins to feel like it could unravel into something much bigger if I don’t stay on top of it. So yes, it’s many different parts of life, not just one specific problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things because of these worries, even after you already know they’re taken care of?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of myself as doing a lot of, you know, ritual-type checking. I mean, I might look back at something once or twice if I’m unsure, like a date or whether I wrote something down correctly, but I don’t know that I repeatedly check things over and over after I truly know they’re handled.\n\nIt’s more that the thought keeps coming back in my mind. So even if something is technically taken care of, I may still keep thinking, “But what if I missed something?” I don’t always go and check again, though. Sometimes I just sit with that uneasy feeling and go over it mentally, which is frustrating in its own way.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a particular stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it did, at least that’s when I really noticed it becoming this constant thing. It’s been about seven months now. My friend died, and shortly after that I was trying to keep our projects moving, sort of not let everything fall apart or disappear, and I think that’s when the worrying started to take up much more space in my head.\n\nAt first I thought, well, of course I’m unsettled, there was a lot going on and it mattered to me. But then it just didn’t really settle back down. Even after the immediate things were handled, my mind stayed in that mode of trying to anticipate every possible problem. So yes, I’d say it began shortly after her death and everything I was trying to keep going afterward.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes… that’s really the part that made me feel like I should come in, honestly. Once the worrying gets started, I can’t seem to turn it off. I’ll try to be reasonable with myself — I’ll say, “All right, you’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can do for now.” And for a few moments that helps, but then the thought sort of circles back around.

I’ll try to be reasonable with myself — I’ll say, “All right, you’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can do for now.” And for a few moments that helps, but then the thought sort of circles back around.\n\nIt feels like my mind doesn’t accept that things are handled. I can know, logically, that I’ve done all I reasonably can, but there’s still this feeling of, “What if there’s something I forgot?” or “What if I haven’t thought far enough ahead?” And then I’m back in it again. Yes, I do. Not always in some dramatic way, but I notice it in my body more than I’d like. I’ll be sitting in my own living room, where there’s really no reason to feel on edge, and I feel sort of wound up, like I can’t quite settle into the chair.

It’s like my body wants to be doing something, even if there isn’t really anything useful to do.

So yes, I’d say it began shortly after her death and everything I was trying to keep going afterward.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, do you find it hard to control or stop it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s really the part that made me feel like I should come in, honestly. Once the worrying gets started, I can’t seem to turn it off. I’ll try to be reasonable with myself — I’ll say, “All right, you’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can do for now.” And for a few moments that helps, but then the thought sort of circles back around.\n\nIt feels like my mind doesn’t accept that things are handled. I can know, logically, that I’ve done all I reasonably can, but there’s still this feeling of, “What if there’s something I forgot?” or “What if I haven’t thought far enough ahead?” And then I’m back in it again. I don’t mean to keep going over it, but it’s hard to stop once it catches hold.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or keyed up when you’re worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not always in some dramatic way, but I notice it in my body more than I’d like. I’ll be sitting in my own living room, where there’s really no reason to feel on edge, and I feel sort of wound up, like I can’t quite settle into the chair.\n\nIf I’m on the phone about something even mildly stressful, I’ll catch myself tapping my fingers, or shifting around, or standing up when I don’t need to. Sometimes I get up and straighten a pillow or move papers around, not because it actually matters, but because just sitting quietly with the worry makes me feel jumpy. It’s like my body wants to be doing something, even if there isn’t really anything useful to do.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these worries been happening on most days for at least six months?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

I don’t mean to keep going over it, but it’s hard to stop once it catches hold.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or keyed up when you’re worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not always in some dramatic way, but I notice it in my body more than I’d like. I’ll be sitting in my own living room, where there’s really no reason to feel on edge, and I feel sort of wound up, like I can’t quite settle into the chair.\n\nIf I’m on the phone about something even mildly stressful, I’ll catch myself tapping my fingers, or shifting around, or standing up when I don’t need to. Sometimes I get up and straighten a pillow or move papers around, not because it actually matters, but because just sitting quietly with the worry makes me feel jumpy. It’s like my body wants to be doing something, even if there isn’t really anything useful to do.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been happening on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’d say most days. It’s been about seven months now, since shortly after my friend died and I was trying to keep those projects moving. I remember thinking at the beginning that it was just the situation, that once things were more settled I’d feel more like myself again.\n\nBut it didn’t really ease off in that way. It became almost part of the rhythm of my days, if that makes sense — not every single minute, but most days there’s been something my mind latches onto and turns over. So yes, it has definitely been longer than six months.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre your worries mostly about your friend’s death and the projects connected to it, rather than about lots of unrelated parts of life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\nmanifestation: \"My mind keeps roaming from the exhibition schedule to my daughter's travel plans to whether the house gutters need fixing, and small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not only those things. That’s where it seemed to start, or at least that’s the clearest thread I can point to, but it hasn’t stayed neatly in that box.

My mind kind of roams from one thing to the next. One minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether something will fall through there, and then somehow I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans and whether she’ll get delayed or something will go wrong, and then I’ll notice the rain and start wondering whether the gutters on the house need fixing. And then that doesn’t feel like just gutters, it starts to feel like, well, if I ignore it maybe there’ll be water damage, and then repairs, and then a whole expensive mess.

So it’s not only about my friend’s death or the projects connected to it. Those are part of it, definitely, but the worry seems to spread. Small things don’t stay small in my head lately. They start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the main thing is… I can’t seem to turn the worrying off once it gets going. I’ll tell myself, “All right, I’ve made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done what can reasonably be done,” and still my mind just keeps circling back to it. It’s like I know, logically, that I’ve handled things as much as I can, but that doesn’t make the worry stop. It just keeps running in the background, and sometimes right in the foreground, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly one specific problem?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It isn’t just one thing, like… if it were only one bill or one medical appointment or something, I could almost understand it. But my mind sort of wanders from one concern to the next. I’ll be thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether everything is lined up properly, and then somehow I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans, whether she’ll make connections safely, whether something will go wrong, and then I’ll notice the weather and think, oh, the gutters on the house probably need attention, and what if that turns into damage, and then that becomes a whole other thread.\n\nIt’s not always enormous things at first. Sometimes it starts with something fairly ordinary, but in my head it begins to feel like it could unravel into something much bigger if I don’t stay on top of it. I mean, I might look back at something once or twice if I’m unsure, like a date or whether I wrote something down correctly, but I don’t know that I repeatedly check things over and over after I truly know they’re handled.\n\nIt’s more that the thought keeps coming back in my mind. They start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having trouble sleeping?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I do sleep, so it’s not as if I’m up all night pacing the house or anything like that. But I haven’t really been keeping track of it in a careful way.

Some nights I notice I’m not settling down as easily, but then other nights I think I’m probably sleeping more or less normally. It’s hard for me to separate what’s actually “trouble sleeping” from just being 67 and waking up sometimes, or having an active mind at bedtime. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.

So… I guess I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed a major sleep problem, but I’m not entirely sure.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the main thing is… I can’t seem to turn the worrying off once it gets going. I’ll tell myself, “All right, I’ve made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done what can reasonably be done,” and still my mind just keeps circling back to it. It’s like I know, logically, that I’ve handled things as much as I can, but that doesn’t make the worry stop. It just keeps running in the background, and sometimes right in the foreground, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly one specific problem?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It isn’t just one thing, like… if it were only one bill or one medical appointment or something, I could almost understand it. But my mind sort of wanders from one concern to the next. I’ll be thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether everything is lined up properly, and then somehow I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans, whether she’ll make connections safely, whether something will go wrong, and then I’ll notice the weather and think, oh, the gutters on the house probably need attention, and what if that turns into damage, and then that becomes a whole other thread.\n\nIt’s not always enormous things at first. Sometimes it starts with something fairly ordinary, but in my head it begins to feel like it could unravel into something much bigger if I don’t stay on top of it. So yes, it’s many different parts of life, not just one specific problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things because of these worries, even after you already know they’re taken care of?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of myself as doing a lot of, you know, ritual-type checking. I mean, I might look back at something once or twice if I’m unsure, like a date or whether I wrote something down correctly, but I don’t know that I repeatedly check things over and over after I truly know they’re handled.\n\nIt’s more that the thought keeps coming back in my mind. So even if something is technically taken care of, I may still keep thinking, “But what if I missed something?” I don’t always go and check again, though. Sometimes I just sit with that uneasy feeling and go over it mentally, which is frustrating in its own way.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a particular stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it did, at least that’s when I really noticed it becoming this constant thing. It’s been about seven months now. My friend died, and shortly after that I was trying to keep our projects moving, sort of not let everything fall apart or disappear, and I think that’s when the worrying started to take up much more space in my head.\n\nAt first I thought, well, of course I’m unsettled, there was a lot going on and it mattered to me. But then it just didn’t really settle back down. Even after the immediate things were handled, my mind stayed in that mode of trying to anticipate every possible problem. So yes, I’d say it began shortly after her death and everything I was trying to keep going afterward.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, do you find it hard to control or stop it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s really the part that made me feel like I should come in, honestly. Once the worrying gets started, I can’t seem to turn it off. I’ll try to be reasonable with myself — I’ll say, “All right, you’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can do for now.” And for a few moments that helps, but then the thought sort of circles back around.\n\nIt feels like my mind doesn’t accept that things are handled. I can know, logically, that I’ve done all I reasonably can, but there’s still this feeling of, “What if there’s something I forgot?” or “What if I haven’t thought far enough ahead?” And then I’m back in it again. I don’t mean to keep going over it, but it’s hard to stop once it catches hold.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or keyed up when you’re worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not always in some dramatic way, but I notice it in my body more than I’d like. I’ll be sitting in my own living room, where there’s really no reason to feel on edge, and I feel sort of wound up, like I can’t quite settle into the chair.\n\nIf I’m on the phone about something even mildly stressful, I’ll catch myself tapping my fingers, or shifting around, or standing up when I don’t need to. Sometimes I get up and straighten a pillow or move papers around, not because it actually matters, but because just sitting quietly with the worry makes me feel jumpy. It’s like my body wants to be doing something, even if there isn’t really anything useful to do.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been happening on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’d say most days. It’s been about seven months now, since shortly after my friend died and I was trying to keep those projects moving. I remember thinking at the beginning that it was just the situation, that once things were more settled I’d feel more like myself again.\n\nBut it didn’t really ease off in that way. It became almost part of the rhythm of my days, if that makes sense — not every single minute, but most days there’s been something my mind latches onto and turns over. So yes, it has definitely been longer than six months.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about your friend’s death and the projects connected to it, rather than about lots of unrelated parts of life?\n\nPatient: No, not only those things. That’s where it seemed to start, or at least that’s the clearest thread I can point to, but it hasn’t stayed neatly in that box.\n\nMy mind kind of roams from one thing to the next. One minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether something will fall through there, and then somehow I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans and whether she’ll get delayed or something will go wrong, and then I’ll notice the rain and start wondering whether the gutters on the house need fixing. And then that doesn’t feel like just gutters, it starts to feel like, well, if I ignore it maybe there’ll be water damage, and then repairs, and then a whole expensive mess.\n\nSo it’s not only about my friend’s death or the projects connected to it. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. More than I would expect, really. It’s not dramatic like I’m collapsing or anything, but I feel worn out very easily.

Even on nights when I think I’ve slept all the way through, I can wake up feeling as though I’ve already put in half a day before I’ve even properly started. And then I’ll do something ordinary — water the garden beds, or sort through papers for book club, something that shouldn’t be a major undertaking — and afterward I find I need to sit down and just gather myself.

It’s a bit frustrating, because these are not strenuous things. They’re the normal little tasks of my day. But lately they seem to take more out of me than they used to.

It’s like I know, logically, that I’ve handled things as much as I can, but that doesn’t make the worry stop. It just keeps running in the background, and sometimes right in the foreground, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly one specific problem?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It isn’t just one thing, like… if it were only one bill or one medical appointment or something, I could almost understand it. But my mind sort of wanders from one concern to the next. I’ll be thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether everything is lined up properly, and then somehow I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans, whether she’ll make connections safely, whether something will go wrong, and then I’ll notice the weather and think, oh, the gutters on the house probably need attention, and what if that turns into damage, and then that becomes a whole other thread.\n\nIt’s not always enormous things at first. Sometimes it starts with something fairly ordinary, but in my head it begins to feel like it could unravel into something much bigger if I don’t stay on top of it. So yes, it’s many different parts of life, not just one specific problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things because of these worries, even after you already know they’re taken care of?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of myself as doing a lot of, you know, ritual-type checking. I mean, I might look back at something once or twice if I’m unsure, like a date or whether I wrote something down correctly, but I don’t know that I repeatedly check things over and over after I truly know they’re handled.\n\nIt’s more that the thought keeps coming back in my mind. So even if something is technically taken care of, I may still keep thinking, “But what if I missed something?” I don’t always go and check again, though. Sometimes I just sit with that uneasy feeling and go over it mentally, which is frustrating in its own way.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a particular stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it did, at least that’s when I really noticed it becoming this constant thing. It’s been about seven months now. My friend died, and shortly after that I was trying to keep our projects moving, sort of not let everything fall apart or disappear, and I think that’s when the worrying started to take up much more space in my head.\n\nAt first I thought, well, of course I’m unsettled, there was a lot going on and it mattered to me. But then it just didn’t really settle back down. Even after the immediate things were handled, my mind stayed in that mode of trying to anticipate every possible problem. So yes, I’d say it began shortly after her death and everything I was trying to keep going afterward.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, do you find it hard to control or stop it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s really the part that made me feel like I should come in, honestly. Once the worrying gets started, I can’t seem to turn it off. I’ll try to be reasonable with myself — I’ll say, “All right, you’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can do for now.” And for a few moments that helps, but then the thought sort of circles back around.\n\nIt feels like my mind doesn’t accept that things are handled. I can know, logically, that I’ve done all I reasonably can, but there’s still this feeling of, “What if there’s something I forgot?” or “What if I haven’t thought far enough ahead?” And then I’m back in it again. I don’t mean to keep going over it, but it’s hard to stop once it catches hold.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or keyed up when you’re worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not always in some dramatic way, but I notice it in my body more than I’d like. I’ll be sitting in my own living room, where there’s really no reason to feel on edge, and I feel sort of wound up, like I can’t quite settle into the chair.\n\nIf I’m on the phone about something even mildly stressful, I’ll catch myself tapping my fingers, or shifting around, or standing up when I don’t need to. Sometimes I get up and straighten a pillow or move papers around, not because it actually matters, but because just sitting quietly with the worry makes me feel jumpy. It’s like my body wants to be doing something, even if there isn’t really anything useful to do.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been happening on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’d say most days. It became almost part of the rhythm of my days, if that makes sense — not every single minute, but most days there’s been something my mind latches onto and turns over. One minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether something will fall through there, and then somehow I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans and whether she’ll get delayed or something will go wrong, and then I’ll notice the rain and start wondering whether the gutters on the house need fixing. And then that doesn’t feel like just gutters, it starts to feel like, well, if I ignore it maybe there’ll be water damage, and then repairs, and then a whole expensive mess.\n\nSo it’s not only about my friend’s death or the projects connected to it. Those are part of it, definitely, but the worry seems to spread. Small things don’t stay small in my head lately. They start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I do sleep, so it’s not as if I’m up all night pacing the house or anything like that. It’s hard for me to separate what’s actually “trouble sleeping” from just being 67 and waking up sometimes, or having an active mind at bedtime. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed a major sleep problem, but I’m not entirely sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily tired?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. More than I would expect, really. It’s not dramatic like I’m collapsing or anything, but I feel worn out very easily.\n\nEven on nights when I think I’ve slept all the way through, I can wake up feeling as though I’ve already put in half a day before I’ve even properly started. And then I’ll do something ordinary — water the garden beds, or sort through papers for book club, something that shouldn’t be a major undertaking — and afterward I find I need to sit down and just gather myself.\n\nIt’s a bit frustrating, because these are not strenuous things. They’re the normal little tasks of my day. But lately they seem to take more out of me than they used to.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas this worrying been interfering with your daily life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I still keep up with the garden and book club, but I’ve started arriving less prepared and turning down parts of the exhibition planning because it feels harder to manage than it used to.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think it has, though maybe not in a completely obvious way from the outside. I’m still doing the things I normally do. I still keep up with the garden, and I still go to book club, so it’s not as if everything has stopped. But I can tell I’m not quite managing things the way I used to.

With book club, for example, I’ll arrive having read less carefully than I meant to, or I’ll feel as though I’m sort of catching up while I’m there rather than coming in with my thoughts organized. And with the exhibition planning, I’ve started turning down pieces of it — not the whole thing, but certain parts — because they feel harder to manage than they should. Even things that used to feel manageable now seem to require this extra mental effort.

So yes, it is interfering some. Not catastrophically, but enough that I notice I’m pulling back in places and not feeling as prepared or capable as I normally would.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.2, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the main thing is… I can’t seem to turn the worrying off once it gets going. I’ll tell myself, “All right, I’ve made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done what can reasonably be done,” and still my mind just keeps circling back to it. It’s like I know, logically, that I’ve handled things as much as I can, but that doesn’t make the worry stop. It just keeps running in the background, and sometimes right in the foreground, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly one specific problem?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It isn’t just one thing, like… if it were only one bill or one medical appointment or something, I could almost understand it. But my mind sort of wanders from one concern to the next. I’ll be thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether everything is lined up properly, and then somehow I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans, whether she’ll make connections safely, whether something will go wrong, and then I’ll notice the weather and think, oh, the gutters on the house probably need attention, and what if that turns into damage, and then that becomes a whole other thread.\n\nIt’s not always enormous things at first. Sometimes it starts with something fairly ordinary, but in my head it begins to feel like it could unravel into something much bigger if I don’t stay on top of it. So yes, it’s many different parts of life, not just one specific problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things because of these worries, even after you already know they’re taken care of?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of myself as doing a lot of, you know, ritual-type checking. I mean, I might look back at something once or twice if I’m unsure, like a date or whether I wrote something down correctly, but I don’t know that I repeatedly check things over and over after I truly know they’re handled.\n\nIt’s more that the thought keeps coming back in my mind. So even if something is technically taken care of, I may still keep thinking, “But what if I missed something?” I don’t always go and check again, though. Sometimes I just sit with that uneasy feeling and go over it mentally, which is frustrating in its own way.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a particular stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it did, at least that’s when I really noticed it becoming this constant thing. It’s been about seven months now. My friend died, and shortly after that I was trying to keep our projects moving, sort of not let everything fall apart or disappear, and I think that’s when the worrying started to take up much more space in my head.\n\nAt first I thought, well, of course I’m unsettled, there was a lot going on and it mattered to me. But then it just didn’t really settle back down. Even after the immediate things were handled, my mind stayed in that mode of trying to anticipate every possible problem. So yes, I’d say it began shortly after her death and everything I was trying to keep going afterward.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, do you find it hard to control or stop it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s really the part that made me feel like I should come in, honestly. Once the worrying gets started, I can’t seem to turn it off. I’ll try to be reasonable with myself — I’ll say, “All right, you’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can do for now.” And for a few moments that helps, but then the thought sort of circles back around.\n\nIt feels like my mind doesn’t accept that things are handled. I can know, logically, that I’ve done all I reasonably can, but there’s still this feeling of, “What if there’s something I forgot?” or “What if I haven’t thought far enough ahead?” And then I’m back in it again. I don’t mean to keep going over it, but it’s hard to stop once it catches hold.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or keyed up when you’re worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not always in some dramatic way, but I notice it in my body more than I’d like. I’ll be sitting in my own living room, where there’s really no reason to feel on edge, and I feel sort of wound up, like I can’t quite settle into the chair.\n\nIf I’m on the phone about something even mildly stressful, I’ll catch myself tapping my fingers, or shifting around, or standing up when I don’t need to. Sometimes I get up and straighten a pillow or move papers around, not because it actually matters, but because just sitting quietly with the worry makes me feel jumpy. It’s like my body wants to be doing something, even if there isn’t really anything useful to do.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been happening on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’d say most days. It’s been about seven months now, since shortly after my friend died and I was trying to keep those projects moving. I remember thinking at the beginning that it was just the situation, that once things were more settled I’d feel more like myself again.\n\nBut it didn’t really ease off in that way. It became almost part of the rhythm of my days, if that makes sense — not every single minute, but most days there’s been something my mind latches onto and turns over. So yes, it has definitely been longer than six months.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about your friend’s death and the projects connected to it, rather than about lots of unrelated parts of life?\n\nPatient: No, not only those things. That’s where it seemed to start, or at least that’s the clearest thread I can point to, but it hasn’t stayed neatly in that box.\n\nMy mind kind of roams from one thing to the next. One minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether something will fall through there, and then somehow I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans and whether she’ll get delayed or something will go wrong, and then I’ll notice the rain and start wondering whether the gutters on the house need fixing. And then that doesn’t feel like just gutters, it starts to feel like, well, if I ignore it maybe there’ll be water damage, and then repairs, and then a whole expensive mess.\n\nSo it’s not only about my friend’s death or the projects connected to it. Those are part of it, definitely, but the worry seems to spread. Small things don’t stay small in my head lately. They start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I do sleep, so it’s not as if I’m up all night pacing the house or anything like that. But I haven’t really been keeping track of it in a careful way.\n\nSome nights I notice I’m not settling down as easily, but then other nights I think I’m probably sleeping more or less normally. It’s hard for me to separate what’s actually “trouble sleeping” from just being 67 and waking up sometimes, or having an active mind at bedtime. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed a major sleep problem, but I’m not entirely sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily tired?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. More than I would expect, really. It’s not dramatic like I’m collapsing or anything, but I feel worn out very easily.\n\nEven on nights when I think I’ve slept all the way through, I can wake up feeling as though I’ve already put in half a day before I’ve even properly started. And then I’ll do something ordinary — water the garden beds, or sort through papers for book club, something that shouldn’t be a major undertaking — and afterward I find I need to sit down and just gather myself.\n\nIt’s a bit frustrating, because these are not strenuous things.   },

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I do sleep, so it’s not as if I’m up all night pacing the house or anything like that. But I haven’t really been keeping track of it in a careful way.\n\nSome nights I notice I’m not settling down as easily, but then other nights I think I’m probably sleeping more or less normally. It’s hard for me to separate what’s actually “trouble sleeping” from just being 67 and waking up sometimes, or having an active mind at bedtime. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed a major sleep problem, but I’m not entirely sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily tired?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. More than I would expect, really. It’s not dramatic like I’m collapsing or anything, but I feel worn out very easily.\n\nEven on nights when I think I’ve slept all the way through, I can wake up feeling as though I’ve already put in half a day before I’ve even properly started. And then I’ll do something ordinary — water the garden beds, or sort through papers for book club, something that shouldn’t be a major undertaking — and afterward I find I need to sit down and just gather myself.\n\nIt’s a bit frustrating, because these are not strenuous things. They’re the normal little tasks of my day. But lately they seem to take more out of me than they used to.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has, though maybe not in a completely obvious way from the outside. I’m still doing the things I normally do. I still keep up with the garden, and I still go to book club, so it’s not as if everything has stopped. But I can tell I’m not quite managing things the way I used to.\n\nWith book club, for example, I’ll arrive having read less carefully than I meant to, or I’ll feel as though I’m sort of catching up while I’m there rather than coming in with my thoughts organized. And with the exhibition planning, I’ve started turning down pieces of it — not the whole thing, but certain parts — because they feel harder to manage than they should. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it began very much as a reaction to my friend’s death, yes. At least, that’s when I first remember it taking hold in this more persistent way. It was shortly after she died, and I was trying to keep our projects moving — things we had both cared about — and at first I told myself, well, of course I’m unsettled, of course I’m worried, there’s been a loss and now there are all these loose ends.

But it’s been about seven months now, and I suppose that’s what makes me hesitate. It still feels connected to her death in some ways, because that was the start of it, but it doesn’t feel only like a direct reaction anymore. It’s more as if the worrying got started then and has just continued on, attaching itself to the projects and responsibilities and whether I’m handling things properly. So yes, it started after she died, but now it feels like it has carried on past the immediate aftermath.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the main thing is… I can’t seem to turn the worrying off once it gets going. I’ll tell myself, “All right, I’ve made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done what can reasonably be done,” and still my mind just keeps circling back to it. It’s like I know, logically, that I’ve handled things as much as I can, but that doesn’t make the worry stop. It just keeps running in the background, and sometimes right in the foreground, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly one specific problem?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It isn’t just one thing, like… if it were only one bill or one medical appointment or something, I could almost understand it. But my mind sort of wanders from one concern to the next. I’ll be thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether everything is lined up properly, and then somehow I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans, whether she’ll make connections safely, whether something will go wrong, and then I’ll notice the weather and think, oh, the gutters on the house probably need attention, and what if that turns into damage, and then that becomes a whole other thread.\n\nIt’s not always enormous things at first. Sometimes it starts with something fairly ordinary, but in my head it begins to feel like it could unravel into something much bigger if I don’t stay on top of it. So yes, it’s many different parts of life, not just one specific problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things because of these worries, even after you already know they’re taken care of?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of myself as doing a lot of, you know, ritual-type checking. I mean, I might look back at something once or twice if I’m unsure, like a date or whether I wrote something down correctly, but I don’t know that I repeatedly check things over and over after I truly know they’re handled.\n\nIt’s more that the thought keeps coming back in my mind. So even if something is technically taken care of, I may still keep thinking, “But what if I missed something?” I don’t always go and check again, though. Sometimes I just sit with that uneasy feeling and go over it mentally, which is frustrating in its own way.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a particular stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it did, at least that’s when I really noticed it becoming this constant thing. It’s been about seven months now. My friend died, and shortly after that I was trying to keep our projects moving, sort of not let everything fall apart or disappear, and I think that’s when the worrying started to take up much more space in my head.\n\nAt first I thought, well, of course I’m unsettled, there was a lot going on and it mattered to me. But then it just didn’t really settle back down. Even after the immediate things were handled, my mind stayed in that mode of trying to anticipate every possible problem. So yes, I’d say it began shortly after her death and everything I was trying to keep going afterward.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, do you find it hard to control or stop it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s really the part that made me feel like I should come in, honestly. Once the worrying gets started, I can’t seem to turn it off. I’ll try to be reasonable with myself — I’ll say, “All right, you’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can do for now.” And for a few moments that helps, but then the thought sort of circles back around.\n\nIt feels like my mind doesn’t accept that things are handled. I can know, logically, that I’ve done all I reasonably can, but there’s still this feeling of, “What if there’s something I forgot?” or “What if I haven’t thought far enough ahead?” And then I’m back in it again. I don’t mean to keep going over it, but it’s hard to stop once it catches hold.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or keyed up when you’re worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not always in some dramatic way, but I notice it in my body more than I’d like. I’ll be sitting in my own living room, where there’s really no reason to feel on edge, and I feel sort of wound up, like I can’t quite settle into the chair.\n\nIf I’m on the phone about something even mildly stressful, I’ll catch myself tapping my fingers, or shifting around, or standing up when I don’t need to. Sometimes I get up and straighten a pillow or move papers around, not because it actually matters, but because just sitting quietly with the worry makes me feel jumpy. It’s like my body wants to be doing something, even if there isn’t really anything useful to do.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been happening on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’d say most days. It’s been about seven months now, since shortly after my friend died and I was trying to keep those projects moving. I remember thinking at the beginning that it was just the situation, that once things were more settled I’d feel more like myself again.\n\nBut it didn’t really ease off in that way. It became almost part of the rhythm of my days, if that makes sense — not every single minute, but most days there’s been something my mind latches onto and turns over. So yes, it has definitely been longer than six months.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about your friend’s death and the projects connected to it, rather than about lots of unrelated parts of life?\n\nPatient: No, not only those things. And then that doesn’t feel like just gutters, it starts to feel like, well, if I ignore it maybe there’ll be water damage, and then repairs, and then a whole expensive mess.\n\nSo it’s not only about my friend’s death or the projects connected to it. Those are part of it, definitely, but the worry seems to spread. Small things don’t stay small in my head lately. They start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. It was shortly after she died, and I was trying to keep our projects moving — things we had both cared about — and at first I told myself, well, of course I’m unsettled, of course I’m worried, there’s been a loss and now there are all these loose ends.\n\nBut it’s been about seven months now, and I suppose that’s what makes me hesitate. It still feels connected to her death in some ways, because that was the start of it, but it doesn’t feel only like a direct reaction anymore. It’s more as if the worrying got started then and has just continued on, attaching itself to the projects and responsibilities and whether I’m handling things properly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it probably is more than the situation calls for, though I don’t always recognize that in the moment.

It’s not that there’s nothing real to think about — there are schedules to keep straight, and family things, and the house always has something that needs attention. But my mind doesn’t seem to just consider them and put them aside. It roams from one thing to the next. I’ll be thinking about the exhibition schedule, and then suddenly I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, and then I’m thinking, “Oh, what about the gutters on the house, did I ever have those looked at properly?” And each thing starts to feel as if, if I don’t stay on top of it immediately, it could somehow unravel into something much bigger.

When I say it out loud, I can hear that some of it is probably out of proportion. A gutter repair is a gutter repair. A travel plan is usually just a travel plan. But in my head, it can feel like one missed detail will set off a whole chain of problems. So yes, I do think the level of worry is more than the actual situation calls for.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.2, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the main thing is… I can’t seem to turn the worrying off once it gets going. I’ll tell myself, “All right, I’ve made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done what can reasonably be done,” and still my mind just keeps circling back to it. It’s like I know, logically, that I’ve handled things as much as I can, but that doesn’t make the worry stop. It just keeps running in the background, and sometimes right in the foreground, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly one specific problem?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It isn’t just one thing, like… if it were only one bill or one medical appointment or something, I could almost understand it. But my mind sort of wanders from one concern to the next. I’ll be thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether everything is lined up properly, and then somehow I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans, whether she’ll make connections safely, whether something will go wrong, and then I’ll notice the weather and think, oh, the gutters on the house probably need attention, and what if that turns into damage, and then that becomes a whole other thread.\n\nIt’s not always enormous things at first. Sometimes it starts with something fairly ordinary, but in my head it begins to feel like it could unravel into something much bigger if I don’t stay on top of it. So yes, it’s many different parts of life, not just one specific problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things because of these worries, even after you already know they’re taken care of?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of myself as doing a lot of, you know, ritual-type checking. I mean, I might look back at something once or twice if I’m unsure, like a date or whether I wrote something down correctly, but I don’t know that I repeatedly check things over and over after I truly know they’re handled.\n\nIt’s more that the thought keeps coming back in my mind. So even if something is technically taken care of, I may still keep thinking, “But what if I missed something?” I don’t always go and check again, though. Sometimes I just sit with that uneasy feeling and go over it mentally, which is frustrating in its own way.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a particular stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it did, at least that’s when I really noticed it becoming this constant thing. It’s been about seven months now. My friend died, and shortly after that I was trying to keep our projects moving, sort of not let everything fall apart or disappear, and I think that’s when the worrying started to take up much more space in my head.\n\nAt first I thought, well, of course I’m unsettled, there was a lot going on and it mattered to me. But then it just didn’t really settle back down. Even after the immediate things were handled, my mind stayed in that mode of trying to anticipate every possible problem. So yes, I’d say it began shortly after her death and everything I was trying to keep going afterward.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, do you find it hard to control or stop it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s really the part that made me feel like I should come in, honestly. Once the worrying gets started, I can’t seem to turn it off. I’ll try to be reasonable with myself — I’ll say, “All right, you’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can do for now.” And for a few moments that helps, but then the thought sort of circles back around.\n\nIt feels like my mind doesn’t accept that things are handled. I can know, logically, that I’ve done all I reasonably can, but there’s still this feeling of, “What if there’s something I forgot?” or “What if I haven’t thought far enough ahead?” And then I’m back in it again. I don’t mean to keep going over it, but it’s hard to stop once it catches hold.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or keyed up when you’re worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not always in some dramatic way, but I notice it in my body more than I’d like. I’ll be sitting in my own living room, where there’s really no reason to feel on edge, and I feel sort of wound up, like I can’t quite settle into the chair.\n\nIf I’m on the phone about something even mildly stressful, I’ll catch myself tapping my fingers, or shifting around, or standing up when I don’t need to. Sometimes I get up and straighten a pillow or move papers around, not because it actually matters, but because just sitting quietly with the worry makes me feel jumpy. It’s like my body wants to be doing something, even if there isn’t really anything useful to do.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been happening on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’d say most days. It’s been about seven months now, since shortly after my friend died and I was trying to keep those projects moving. I remember thinking at the beginning that it was just the situation, that once things were more settled I’d feel more like myself again.\n\nBut it didn’t really ease off in that way. It became almost part of the rhythm of my days, if that makes sense — not every single minute, but most days there’s been something my mind latches onto and turns over. So yes, it has definitely been longer than six months.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about your friend’s death and the projects connected to it, rather than about lots of unrelated parts of life?\n\nPatient: No, not only those things. That’s where it seemed to start, or at least that’s the clearest thread I can point to, but it hasn’t stayed neatly in that box.\n\nMy mind kind of roams from one thing to the next. One minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether something will fall through there, and then somehow I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans and whether she’ll get delayed or something will go wrong, and then I’ll notice the rain and start wondering whether the gutters on the house need fixing. And then that doesn’t feel like just gutters, it starts to feel like, well, if I ignore it maybe there’ll be water damage, and then repairs, and then a whole expensive mess.\n\nSo it’s not only about my friend’s death or the projects connected to it. Those are part of it, definitely, but the worry seems to spread. Small things don’t stay small in my head lately. They start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I do sleep, so it’s not as if I’m up all night pacing the house or anything like that. But I haven’t really been keeping track of it in a careful way.\n\nSome nights I notice I’m not settling down as easily, but then other nights I think I’m probably sleeping more or less normally. It’s hard for me to separate what’s actually “trouble sleeping” from just being 67 and waking up sometimes, or having an active mind at bedtime. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed a major sleep problem, but I’m not entirely sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily tired?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. More than I would expect, really. It’s not dramatic like I’m collapsing or anything, but I feel worn out very easily.\n\nEven on nights when I think I’ve slept all the way through, I can wake up feeling as though I’ve already put in half a day before I’ve even properly started. And then I’ll do something ordinary — water the garden beds, or sort through papers for book club, something that shouldn’t be a major undertaking — and afterward I find I need to sit down and just gather myself.\n\nIt’s a bit frustrating, because these are not strenuous things. They’re the normal little tasks of my day. But lately they seem to take more out of me than they used to.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has, though maybe not in a completely obvious way from the outside. I’m still doing the things I normally do. I still keep up with the garden, and I still go to book club, so it’s not as if everything has stopped. But I can tell I’m not quite managing things the way I used to.\n\nWith book club, for example, I’ll arrive having read less carefully than I meant to, or I’ll feel as though I’m sort of catching up while I’m there rather than coming in with my thoughts organized. And with the exhibition planning, I’ve started turning down pieces of it — not the whole thing, but certain parts — because they feel harder to manage than they should. Even things that used to feel manageable now seem to require this extra mental effort.\n\nSo yes, it is interfering some. Not catastrophically, but enough that I notice I’m pulling back in places and not feeling as prepared or capable as I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worrying is mainly a reaction to your friend’s death and its aftermath, or does it now feel separate from that event?\n\nPatient: I think it began very much as a reaction to my friend’s death, yes. At least, that’s when I first remember it taking hold in this more persistent way. It was shortly after she died, and I was trying to keep our projects moving — things we had both cared about — and at first I told myself, well, of course I’m unsettled, of course I’m worried, there’s been a loss and now there are all these loose ends.\n\nBut it’s been about seven months now, and I suppose that’s what makes me hesitate. It still feels connected to her death in some ways, because that was the start of it, but it doesn’t feel only like a direct reaction anymore. It’s more as if the worrying got started then and has just continued on, attaching itself to the projects and responsibilities and whether I’m handling things properly. So yes, it started after she died, but now it feels like it has carried on past the immediate aftermath.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is more than the situation actually calls for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is more than the situation calls for, though I don’t always recognize that in the moment.\n\nIt’s not that there’s nothing real to think about — there are schedules to keep straight, and family things, and the house always has something that needs attention. I’ll be sitting in my own living room, where there’s really no reason to feel on edge, and I feel sort of wound up, like I can’t quite settle into the chair.\n\nIf I’m on the phone about something even mildly stressful, I’ll catch myself tapping my fingers, or shifting around, or standing up when I don’t need to. Sometimes I get up and straighten a pillow or move papers around, not because it actually matters, but because just sitting quietly with the worry makes me feel jumpy. It’s like my body wants to be doing something, even if there isn’t really anything useful to do.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been happening on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’d say most days. It’s been about seven months now, since shortly after my friend died and I was trying to keep those projects moving. I remember thinking at the beginning that it was just the situation, that once things were more settled I’d feel more like myself again.\n\nBut it didn’t really ease off in that way. It became almost part of the rhythm of my days, if that makes sense — not every single minute, but most days there’s been something my mind latches onto and turns over. So yes, it has definitely been longer than six months.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about your friend’s death and the projects connected to it, rather than about lots of unrelated parts of life?\n\nPatient: No, not only those things. That’s where it seemed to start, or at least that’s the clearest thread I can point to, but it hasn’t stayed neatly in that box.\n\nMy mind kind of roams from one thing to the next. One minute I’m thinking about the exhibition schedule and whether something will fall through there, and then somehow I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans and whether she’ll get delayed or something will go wrong, and then I’ll notice the rain and start wondering whether the gutters on the house need fixing. And then that doesn’t feel like just gutters, it starts to feel like, well, if I ignore it maybe there’ll be water damage, and then repairs, and then a whole expensive mess.\n\nSo it’s not only about my friend’s death or the projects connected to it. Those are part of it, definitely, but the worry seems to spread. Small things don’t stay small in my head lately. They start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I do sleep, so it’s not as if I’m up all night pacing the house or anything like that. But I haven’t really been keeping track of it in a careful way.\n\nSome nights I notice I’m not settling down as easily, but then other nights I think I’m probably sleeping more or less normally. It’s hard for me to separate what’s actually “trouble sleeping” from just being 67 and waking up sometimes, or having an active mind at bedtime. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed a major sleep problem, but I’m not entirely sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily tired?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. More than I would expect, really. It’s not dramatic like I’m collapsing or anything, but I feel worn out very easily.\n\nEven on nights when I think I’ve slept all the way through, I can wake up feeling as though I’ve already put in half a day before I’ve even properly started. And then I’ll do something ordinary — water the garden beds, or sort through papers for book club, something that shouldn’t be a major undertaking — and afterward I find I need to sit down and just gather myself.\n\nIt’s a bit frustrating, because these are not strenuous things. They’re the normal little tasks of my day. But lately they seem to take more out of me than they used to.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has, though maybe not in a completely obvious way from the outside. I’m still doing the things I normally do. I still keep up with the garden, and I still go to book club, so it’s not as if everything has stopped. But I can tell I’m not quite managing things the way I used to.\n\nWith book club, for example, I’ll arrive having read less carefully than I meant to, or I’ll feel as though I’m sort of catching up while I’m there rather than coming in with my thoughts organized. And with the exhibition planning, I’ve started turning down pieces of it — not the whole thing, but certain parts — because they feel harder to manage than they should. Even things that used to feel manageable now seem to require this extra mental effort.\n\nSo yes, it is interfering some. Not catastrophically, but enough that I notice I’m pulling back in places and not feeling as prepared or capable as I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worrying is mainly a reaction to your friend’s death and its aftermath, or does it now feel separate from that event?\n\nPatient: I think it began very much as a reaction to my friend’s death, yes. At least, that’s when I first remember it taking hold in this more persistent way. It was shortly after she died, and I was trying to keep our projects moving — things we had both cared about — and at first I told myself, well, of course I’m unsettled, of course I’m worried, there’s been a loss and now there are all these loose ends.\n\nBut it’s been about seven months now, and I suppose that’s what makes me hesitate. It still feels connected to her death in some ways, because that was the start of it, but it doesn’t feel only like a direct reaction anymore. It’s more as if the worrying got started then and has just continued on, attaching itself to the projects and responsibilities and whether I’m handling things properly. So yes, it started after she died, but now it feels like it has carried on past the immediate aftermath.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is more than the situation actually calls for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is more than the situation calls for, though I don’t always recognize that in the moment.\n\nIt’s not that there’s nothing real to think about — there are schedules to keep straight, and family things, and the house always has something that needs attention. But my mind doesn’t seem to just consider them and put them aside. It roams from one thing to the next. I’ll be thinking about the exhibition schedule, and then suddenly I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, and then I’m thinking, “Oh, what about the gutters on the house, did I ever have those looked at properly?” And each thing starts to feel as if, if I don’t stay on top of it immediately, it could somehow unravel into something much bigger.\n\nWhen I say it out loud, I can hear that some of it is probably out of proportion. A gutter repair is a gutter repair. A travel plan is usually just a travel plan. But in my head, it can feel like one missed detail will set off a whole chain of problems. So yes, I do think the level of worry is more than the actual situation calls for.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

More than I would expect, really. Not catastrophically, but enough that I notice I’m pulling back in places and not feeling as prepared or capable as I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worrying is mainly a reaction to your friend’s death and its aftermath, or does it now feel separate from that event?\n\nPatient: I think it began very much as a reaction to my friend’s death, yes. At least, that’s when I first remember it taking hold in this more persistent way. It was shortly after she died, and I was trying to keep our projects moving — things we had both cared about — and at first I told myself, well, of course I’m unsettled, of course I’m worried, there’s been a loss and now there are all these loose ends.\n\nBut it’s been about seven months now, and I suppose that’s what makes me hesitate. It still feels connected to her death in some ways, because that was the start of it, but it doesn’t feel only like a direct reaction anymore. It’s more as if the worrying got started then and has just continued on, attaching itself to the projects and responsibilities and whether I’m handling things properly. So yes, it started after she died, but now it feels like it has carried on past the immediate aftermath.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is more than the situation actually calls for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is more than the situation calls for, though I don’t always recognize that in the moment.\n\nIt’s not that there’s nothing real to think about — there are schedules to keep straight, and family things, and the house always has something that needs attention. But my mind doesn’t seem to just consider them and put them aside. It roams from one thing to the next. I’ll be thinking about the exhibition schedule, and then suddenly I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, and then I’m thinking, “Oh, what about the gutters on the house, did I ever have those looked at properly?” And each thing starts to feel as if, if I don’t stay on top of it immediately, it could somehow unravel into something much bigger.\n\nWhen I say it out loud, I can hear that some of it is probably out of proportion. A gutter repair is a gutter repair. A travel plan is usually just a travel plan. But in my head, it can feel like one missed detail will set off a whole chain of problems. So yes, I do think the level of worry is more than the actual situation calls for.\n\nDoctor: Has this ongoing worrying lasted longer than six months after your friend died?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. It’s been about seven months now, if I’m counting from shortly after my friend died. That was when I first noticed it becoming this ongoing thing, especially when I was trying to keep our projects going without her.\n\nAt first I thought, well, of course I’m worried, there’s been a lot to manage. I’m not entirely sure how to answer that. I mean, I haven’t especially thought of it as “trouble concentrating,” if that makes sense. I do find my mind can wander when I’m trying to settle on one thing, but I’m not sure whether that’s the same as not being able to concentrate.
